People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Like during hackathon check-ins or waiting in office-hour lines, I get really antsy. I’ll keep checking the time, shifting around, and I kind of have to stop myself from wanting to jump in before it’s actually my turn.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing tasks and managing your time?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I can get annoyed sometimes, but I haven’t noticed being especially irritable compared to other people.\n\nDoctor: Were you more physically active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so, at least from what I’ve been told. And at home, my parents had to sit with me during homework because I’d get up, wander off, or start doing something else before I finished.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of restlessness and difficulty staying on track for as long as you can remember, even as a teenager?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it’s been there for as long as I can remember. Like even in elementary school, teachers would write that I rushed through worksheets, lost track of the directions, or talked over classmates during group work. Like during hackathon check-ins or waiting in office-hour lines, I get really antsy. I’ll keep checking the time, shifting around, and I kind of have to stop myself from wanting to jump in before it’s actually my turn.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing tasks and managing your time?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I use calendars and reminders a lot, so I can usually keep things together, but I don’t really know if I’m worse at it than other people. I haven’t paid super close attention to that specifically.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble controlling your emotions when you get frustrated or upset?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I get frustrated sometimes like anyone, but I haven’t noticed myself losing control of my emotions in a big way. I’d probably need to think about it more.\n\nDoctor: Do you often get easily annoyed or irritable?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not often. I mean, I can get annoyed sometimes, but I haven’t noticed being especially irritable compared to other people.\n\nDoctor: Were you more physically active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so, at least from what I’ve been told. In elementary school, teachers would write comments about me rushing through worksheets, forgetting the directions, or talking over other kids during group work. And at home, my parents had to sit with me during homework because I’d get up, wander off, or start doing something else before I finished.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of restlessness and difficulty staying on track for as long as you can remember, even as a teenager?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it’s been there for as long as I can remember. Like even in elementary school, teachers would write that I rushed through worksheets, lost track of the directions, or talked over classmates during group work. And at home my parents would sit next to me during homework because I’d wander off or start something else before finishing.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty following through on instructions or finishing tasks at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yes, sometimes. If someone gives me a list of changes or revisions to do, I’ll usually do the first couple right away, but then I kind of drift into tweaking or redesigning something else. Then I’ll realize later I left one small but important change unfinished.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or making mistakes in your work or daily activities due to not paying attention to details?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I’ll think something is finished, like a mockup or a data chart, and then later I’ll notice I mislabeled an axis or used the wrong color key. Sometimes it’s just one tiny instruction in the brief that I somehow missed, even though I thought I’d checked it.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find yourself being more physically active or restless than your peers now that you're an adult?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do still feel restless. Like if I’m on a long Zoom call or I have to sit and work quietly in one spot for a while, I start getting this uncomfortable feeling in my body, like I need to get up and pace or at least do something with my hands.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties with restlessness, staying on track, and finishing tasks caused significant problems in your relationships or work performance?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s not like my whole life is falling apart, but it has caused problems. I’ve lost a couple small freelance contracts because I didn’t follow through cleanly, and I missed out on a portfolio competition for the same kind of reason. Some friends and collaborators are also more hesitant to work with me now, even though they like my ideas, because they’re not sure I’ll finish things reliably.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

It’s been frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty often. In meetings or portfolio reviews I’ll be bouncing my leg, clicking a pen, twisting my rings, or just shifting around in my chair. Like when people are brainstorming, I’ll blurt out my idea before they’re done explaining theirs. Or I’ll start adding things to their Figma board too quickly. I don’t mean to be rude—I’m usually just excited—but afterward I realize I probably came off as pushy.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulses when you're alone, like buying something spontaneously or starting a new project without thinking it through?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I’ll sometimes get an idea and want to jump into it, but I haven’t really paid attention to whether I’m doing it without thinking. (Note: In adolescents or adults, may be limited to feeling restless.)\n  manifestation: If I have to sit still through a long Zoom call or quietly work in one place, I start feeling this uncomfortable pressure in my body like I need to get up, pace, or do something with my hands.\n- key: S013\n  name: Quiet_Activity_Difficulty\n  description: Often unable to play or engage in leisure activities quietly.\n  manifestation: Even when I try to relax by reading or doing a calm sketching exercise, I end up talking to myself, playing music, checking something, or turning it into a bigger project instead of just being quiet with it.\n- key: S014\n  name: Constant_Activity\n  description: Is often 'on the go', acting as if 'driven by a motor'.\n  manifestation: On days when I don’t have classes or a formal schedule, I still jump from editing a prototype to cleaning my desk to opening a new dataset, like resting feels weird unless I’m actively doing something.\n- key: S017\n  name: Waiting_Turn_Difficulty\n  description: Often has difficulty waiting his or her turn.\n  manifestation: Waiting for my turn during hackathon check-ins or office-hour queues makes me really antsy, and I catch myself checking the time, shifting around, or wanting to jump in before it’s actually my turn.\n- key: S018\n  name: Interrupting_Intruding\n  description: Often interrupts or intrudes on others (e.g., butts into conversations, games, or activities).\n  manifestation: When people are brainstorming, I’ll blurt out my idea or start adding to their Figma board before they’ve finished explaining, and I can tell afterward that I came off as pushy even though I was excited.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: Even in elementary school, my teachers wrote comments about me rushing through worksheets, losing track of directions, and talking over classmates during group work. My parents used to sit beside me for homework because I would wander off or start something else before finishing.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I've lost small freelance contracts and missed out on a portfolio competition because people couldn't rely on me to follow through cleanly. It has also made some friends and collaborators hesitant to work with me, even though they like my ideas.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 5 months\n  manifestation: This has been causing real problems for about a year and five months, since I moved from structured school projects into building my freelance UX and data-visualization portfolio.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 19 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S008\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have these difficulties with restlessness, staying on track, and finishing tasks caused significant problems in your relationships or work performance?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s not like my whole life is falling apart, but it has caused problems. I’ve lost a couple small freelance contracts because I didn’t follow through cleanly, and I missed out on a portfolio competition for the same kind of reason. Some friends and collaborators are also more hesitant to work with me now, even though they like my ideas, because they’re not sure I’ll finish things reliably.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these difficulties are causing you a lot of distress or discomfort?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does bother me. I feel embarrassed and frustrated because I’ve lost small freelance contracts and missed out on a portfolio competition when people couldn’t count on me to follow through cleanly. And it’s hard knowing some friends or collaborators still like my ideas, but hesitate to work with me because they’re not sure I’ll finish things reliably.\n\nDoctor: Did you have difficulty controlling your behavior or waiting for your turn in games when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, waiting my turn has been hard for me. Like in hackathon check-ins or office-hour queues, I get really antsy. I’ll keep checking the time, shifting around, and I have this urge to jump in before it’s actually my turn.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you experienced these difficulties with restlessness and staying on track in multiple areas of your life, such as at work, at home, and in social situations?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. (Note: In adolescents or adults, may be limited to feeling restless.)\n  manifestation: If I have to sit still through a long Zoom call or quietly work in one place, I start feeling this uncomfortable pressure in my body like I need to get up, pace, or do something with my hands.\n- key: S013\n  name: Quiet_Activity_Difficulty\n  description: Often unable to play or engage in leisure activities quietly.\n  manifestation: Even when I try to relax by reading or doing a calm sketching exercise, I end up talking to myself, playing music, checking something, or turning it into a bigger project instead of just being quiet with it.\n- key: S014\n  name: Constant_Activity\n  description: Is often 'on the go', acting as if 'driven by a motor'.\n  manifestation: On days when I don’t have classes or a formal schedule, I still jump from editing a prototype to cleaning my desk to opening a new dataset, like resting feels weird unless I’m actively doing something.\n- key: S017\n  name: Waiting_Turn_Difficulty\n  description: Often has difficulty waiting his or her turn.\n  manifestation: Waiting for my turn during hackathon check-ins or office-hour queues makes me really antsy, and I catch myself checking the time, shifting around, or wanting to jump in before it’s actually my turn.\n- key: S018\n  name: Interrupting_Intruding\n  description: Often interrupts or intrudes on others (e.g., butts into conversations, games, or activities).\n  manifestation: When people are brainstorming, I’ll blurt out my idea or start adding to their Figma board before they’ve finished explaining, and I can tell afterward that I came off as pushy even though I was excited.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: Even in elementary school, my teachers wrote comments about me rushing through worksheets, losing track of directions, and talking over classmates during group work. Like in hackathon check-ins or office-hour queues, I get really antsy. I’ll keep checking the time, shifting around, and I have this urge to jump in before it’s actually my turn.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with restlessness and staying on track in multiple areas of your life, such as at work, at home, and in social situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate it out. I don’t mean to be rude—I’m usually just excited—but afterward I realize I probably came off as pushy.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulses when you're alone, like buying something spontaneously or starting a new project without thinking it through?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I’ll sometimes get an idea and want to jump into it, but I haven’t really paid attention to whether I’m doing it without thinking. Like during hackathon check-ins or waiting in office-hour lines, I get really antsy. (Note: In adolescents or adults, may be limited to feeling restless.)\n  manifestation: If I have to sit still through a long Zoom call or quietly work in one place, I start feeling this uncomfortable pressure in my body like I need to get up, pace, or do something with my hands.\n- key: S013\n  name: Quiet_Activity_Difficulty\n  description: Often unable to play or engage in leisure activities quietly.\n  manifestation: Even when I try to relax by reading or doing a calm sketching exercise, I end up talking to myself, playing music, checking something, or turning it into a bigger project instead of just being quiet with it.\n- key: S014\n  name: Constant_Activity\n  description: Is often 'on the go', acting as if 'driven by a motor'.\n  manifestation: On days when I don’t have classes or a formal schedule, I still jump from editing a prototype to cleaning my desk to opening a new dataset, like resting feels weird unless I’m actively doing something.\n- key: S017\n  name: Waiting_Turn_Difficulty\n  description: Often has difficulty waiting his or her turn.\n  manifestation: Waiting for my turn during hackathon check-ins or office-hour queues makes me really antsy, and I catch myself checking the time, shifting around, or wanting to jump in before it’s actually my turn.\n- key: S018\n  name: Interrupting_Intruding\n  description: Often interrupts or intrudes on others (e.g., butts into conversations, games, or activities).\n  manifestation: When people are brainstorming, I’ll blurt out my idea or start adding to their Figma board before they’ve finished explaining, and I can tell afterward that I came off as pushy even though I was excited.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: Even in elementary school, my teachers wrote comments about me rushing through worksheets, losing track of directions, and talking over classmates during group work.
